Finite Sample Analysis of Minimax Offline Reinforcement Learning: Completeness, Fast Rates and First-Order Efficiency
Masatoshi Uehara, Masaaki Imaizumi, Nan Jiang, Nathan Kallus, Wen Sun, Tengyang Xie

Abstract
We offer a theoretical characterization of off-policy evaluation (OPE) in reinforcement learning using function approximation for marginal importance weights and q q -functions when these are estimated using recent minimax methods. Under various combinations of realizability and completeness assumptions, we show that the minimax approach enables us to achieve a fast rate of convergence for weights and quality functions, characterized by the critical inequality ( Bartlett et al. 2005 ) . Based on this result, we analyze convergence rates for OPE. In particular, we introduce novel alternative completeness conditions under which OPE is feasible and we present the first finite-sample result with first-order efficiency in non-tabular environments, i.e., having the minimal coefficient in the leading term.



1
Introduction

Off-policy evaluation (OPE) is the problem of estimating the expected return in an unknown Markov decision process (MDP) of a given decision policy, known as the evaluation policy, using historical data generated by another policy, known as the behavior policy
(
Precup
et al. 2000
;
Thomas
et al. 2015
;
Bibaut et al. 2019
;
Levine
et al. 2020
;
Namkoong et al. 2020
;
Shi
et al. 2021
;
Ma
et al. 2021
)
. OPE is especially important in applications where online experimentation may be costly or dangerous, such as in medicine
(
Luckett et al. 2018
;
Kosorok and
Laber 2019
;
Chakraborty and
Moodie 2013
)
, recommendation systems
(
Chen et al. 2019
)
, and education
(
Mandel et al. 2014
)
. OPE is also utilized as an intermediate step for
off-policy
policy optimization, where the goal is finding a well-performing policy using the historical data
(
Thomas
et al. 2015
;
Lazaric
et al. 2016
)
.

Our goal is to develop estimators for OPE and study their convergence rates.
To give a high-level overview of our results, we briefly introduce our problem setting here, with formal definitions deferred to
Section

2
. We consider an MDP represented by a tuple
M=\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P_{S^{\prime}|S,A},P_{R|S,A},\gamma,d_{0}\}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is a state space,
\mathcal{A}
is an action space,
P_{S^{\prime}\mid S,A}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta(\mathcal{S})
is an
unknown
transition distribution,
P_{R\mid S,A}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta(\mathbb{R})
is an
unknown
reward distribution,
\gamma\in[0,1)
is a discount factor, and
d_{0}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
is an initial state distribution on
\mathcal{S}
. A policy
\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
maps from state to a distribution over
\mathcal{A}
. In OPE, we consider two policies: an evaluation policy
\pi^{\mathrm{e}}
and a behavior policy
\pi^{\mathrm{b}}
. We define the policy value of the evaluation policy
\pi^{\mathrm{e}}
as

\displaystyle J:=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{t}\right],

(1)

where the expectation is taken under the MDP and evaluation policy
\pi^{\mathrm{e}}
. In other words, the above expectation is taken with respect to the distribution over trajectories
s_{0},a_{0},r_{0},s_{1},a_{1},\cdots
given by the product
d_{0}(s_{0})\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(a_{0}|s_{0})P_{R|S,A}(r_{0}|s_{0},a_{0})P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s_{1}|s_{0},a_{0})\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(a_{1}|s_{1})\cdots
.

We wish to estimate
J
based on
n
independent and identically distributed transition observations
generated by the behavior policy
\pi^{\mathrm{b}}
under the same MDP:

\displaystyle\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim P_{S,A,R,S^{\prime}}(s,a,r,s^{\prime})=P_{S}(s)\pi^{\mathrm{b}}(a\mid s)P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)P_{R|S,A}(r\mid s,a).

(2)

where
P_{S}
is some state distribution, possibly different from
d_{0}
.

When
\gamma=0
(and understanding
0^{0}=1
), the OPE problem is reduced to the problem of treatment effect estimation from unconfounded data in causal inference
(
Imbens 2015
;
Chernozhukov
et al. 2018
;
Robins
et al. 1994
)
. However, the reinforcement learning (RL) problem (
\gamma>0
) is more complicated in that we need to deal with the sequential nature of the problem and leverage its Markovianity.

Standard approaches to estimating the policy value
J
use the
q
-function, the policy value when starting at a given state-action pair, or the
w
-function, the density ratio between the evaluation policy’s discounted state-action average occupancy distribution and the state-action distribution of the observed data (see
Section

2
for precise definitions).
These approaches use the fact that for the true
q
-function,
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
, and for the true
w
-function,
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can express
J
as

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0},a\sim\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(\cdot\mid s_{0})}[q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s_{0},a)]=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim P_{S},a\sim\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(\cdot\mid s),r\sim P_{R\mid S,A}(\cdot\mid s,a)}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)r]

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0},a\sim\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(\cdot\mid s_{0})}[q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s_{0},a)]

(3)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\mathbb{E}_{s\sim P_{S},a\sim\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(\cdot\mid s),r\sim P_{R\mid S,A}(\cdot\mid s,a)}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)(r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+\gamma\mathbb{E}_{a^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(\cdot\mid s^{\prime})}[q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})])].

Therefore, we can estimate
J
by estimating
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
and/or
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
from the data and then plugging these in above and replacing true expectations with empirical averages.
Plugging into the reformulation in the first equality yields to the so-called direct method of estimation, the second yields importance sampling
(
Liu
et al. 2018
)
, and the third yields the doubly robust method
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
.

Minimax estimators
have recently attracted significant attention for estimating
q
-functions
(
Antos
et al. 2008
;
Feng
et al. 2019
;
Uehara
et al. 2020
)
and
w
-functions
(
Nachum et al. 2019
;
Zhang
et al. 2020
;
Uehara
et al. 2020
)
.
These minimax estimators are defined as solutions of zero-sum games between two function classes, the function to be learned and a critic function. Minimax estimators are easily amenable to function approximation as one can choose any function class, and models for the MDP itself are never needed.
In our work, we consider a unified formulation for minimax
q
- and
w
-estimation that incorporates the possibility of a
stabilizer
term, which penalizes the norm of the critic function (distinguished from a regularizer, which penalizes the learned function). This unified formulation, in particular, also recovers as special cases many existing estimators, including modified RBM
(
Antos
et al. 2008
)
, the DICE family of estimators
(
Nachum et al. 2019
;
Zhang
et al. 2020
)
, MWL and MQL
(
Uehara
et al. 2020
)
.
Minimax
q
-estimators can be seen as one-step alternative to the multi-step Fitted Q-Iteration (FQI) method
(
Ernst
et al. 2005
;
Le
et al. 2019a
)
, which uses least-squares regression to repeatedly apply backward recursion of values.
For
w
-estimation with general function approximation, the minimax approach is the predominant method.

In this paper, we study minimax estimators of
q
- and
w
-functions and their resulting OPE estimators.
We first answer the following question:

Under what sufficient
conditions can we obtain (non-asymptotic) statistical rates with polynomial dependence on problem-dependent quantities for learning
q
-functions,
w
-functions, and policy value using minimax methods with general function approximation, and how do the rates change with different conditions?

The problem-dependent quantities mentioned here include the effective horizon
1/(1-\gamma)
, an upper bound on the reward
r
, and an upper bound on
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
. We emphasize that a number of standard methods for OPE utilizing importance sampling based on sequential density ratios
(
Jiang and Li 2016
;
Precup
et al. 2000
;
Robins
et al. 1999
;
Thomas and
Brunskill 2016
;
Bibaut et al. 2019
;
Zhang
et al. 2013a
;
Jiang
et al. 2019a
)
do
not
satisfy the polynomial dependence, since their error is exponential in the effective horizon.

In our analyses, two types of expressiveness conditions on the function classes play important roles:
realizability
and
completeness
.
Given hypothesis classes
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{W}
for the
q
- and
w
-functions, respectively, realizability refers to the assumption that they contain the true functions, namely,
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}
, respectively.
Completeness for
q
-functions states that the hypothesis class is closed under the Bellman operator
\mathcal{B}
(precisely defined in Section
2
), i.e., that
\{\mathcal{B}q:q\in\mathbb{Q}\}\subset\mathbb{Q}
holds
(
Chen and
Jiang 2019
;
Munos and
Szepesvári 2008
)
.
Completeness for
w
-functions is a new analogous condition we introduce with respect to the
backward
Bellman operator.
We also introduce new adjoint versions of both
q
- and
w
-completeness. We show how different combinations of these realizability, completeness, and adjoint completeness assumptions provide sufficient conditions and how the rates differ in each case and with or without stabilizer terms.

For
q
-estimation, is is well-known that
q
-realizability and -completeness together yield finite-sample guarantees for FQI
(
Munos and
Szepesvári 2008
;
Fan
et al. 2020
)
. Completeness plays a crucial role in handling the dynamics of the problem, and when we only assume realizability we generally suffer exponential dependence on the effective horizon
(
Wang
et al. 2020
)
, which is undesirable in RL
(
Liu
et al. 2018
;
Kallus and
Uehara 2020a
)
.
Unlike FQI, which only applies to
q
-estimation, statistical properties of minimax estimators are neither well understood nor tightly characterized, despite of their recent popularity. In particular, no work currently derives finite-sample convergence rates for
w
-estimation with general function approximation. For minimax
q
-estimators, although there is some work that derives finite-sample errors
(
Antos
et al. 2008
;
Chen and
Jiang 2019
;
Farahm et al. 2016
)
, their results are either limited to finite hypothesis classes or obtain slow rates of
O(n^{-1/4})
even in settings where
O(n^{-1/2})
rates can be attained, as we will do.
Finally, we obtain rates of convergence under completely new combinations of realizability and completeness conditions, showing that estimation without bad horizon dependence is possible in new regimes.

To tackle this first question, under a variety of realizability and completeness condition combinations,
we establish
fast
rates of convergence of minimax
q
- and
w
-estimators in terms of Bellman residual errors and L2 errors (precisely defined in
Section

5
). The fast rates are characterized by the critical radius of a localized Rademacher complexities of the function classes
(
Bartlett
et al. 2005
)
. This same quantity is often used to tightly characterize fast rates for regression and density estimation
(
Wainwright 2019
)
. The results also satisfy our requirement of polynomial dependence on the problem-dependent quantities. In particular, we obtain the
first
finite-sample result with a fast rate for any
w
-estimator. We also obtain the
first
finite-sample result with a fast rate for minimax
q
-estimators with general function approximation.

A second question we tackle is

Under what sufficient
conditions can we obtain (non-asymptotic) statistical rates with first-order efficiency for minimax policy value estimation?

For OPE in the infinite-horizon setting we study,

(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
derived the semiparametric efficiency bound, which is essentially the smallest-possible value of the limit of
\sqrt{n}
-scaled mean squared error (for more detail on semiparametric efficiency bounds see
(
van der
Vaart 1998
;
Tsiatis 2006
;
Kosorok 2008
)
).
Representing this quantity by
\mathrm{EB}
, we say that an error bound with probability
1-\delta
is first-order efficient if it has the form
\sqrt{2(\mathrm{EB})\log(1/\delta)/n}+o(1/\sqrt{n})
, that is, if the constant on the leading term is the smallest-possible instance-dependent constant for a sub-Gaussian tail.
We provide the first first-order efficient finite-sample guarantees for OPE in general function approximation settings.
In comparison,
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
only showed that the efficiency bound is achievable asymptotically, hiding many important and possibly exponential terms in the higher-order terms, and they rely on the estimation of
q
- and
w
-functions at some rates as a basic assumption, where it is not clear what sufficient conditions may make such estimation possible.
Aside from this, the only first-order efficient guarantees are in the time-varying tabular setting (i.e., finite state and action spaces with time-dependent transition and reward distributions) using model-based estimates
(
Yin and
Wang 2020
)
.
Under realizability and completeness of both
w
- and
q
-functions, we obtain the

first
finite-sample result with first-order efficiency for OPE in general environments with general function approximation.

We next give a brief overview of the paper and the results. We summarize some of their implications in
Table

1
.

•

(
Section

4
) Under
q
- and
w
-realizability, we prove general finite-sample error bounds for estimating policy value using minimax estimators (without stabilizers) by refining the analysis of
Uehara
et al. 2020
. We derive specific rates of convergence depending on the types of hypothesis classes, such as the VC classes, Sobolev balls, and neural networks.

•

(
Sections

6
and
5
)
We relax one of the realizability conditions and impose instead one of a variety of completeness-type conditions. We show minimax estimators (with stabilizers) can achieve a fast rate of convergence in estimating the
q
- and
w
-functions themselves in either Bellman residual error or L2-error.
The rates depends on the specific hypothesis class as above.
We apply these results to derive the rate of policy value estimators, which is generally slower than the rate in
Section

4
.

•

(
Section

7
) Under both realizability and completeness of both
w
- and
q
-functions, we show that the policy value estimator achieves a convergence rate that is generally faster than the cases in
Sections

4
,
6
and
5
. Under mild conditions, we obtain the first non-asymptotic first-order-efficient bound in general settings. As a byproduct, we also obtain the super-fast
O(1/n)
rate for (non-tabular) deterministic MDPs.

•

(
Section

8
) We extend our results to: (i) state-based (instead of state-action-based) minimax estimators
(
Liu
et al. 2018
;
Feng
et al. 2019
)
and (ii) off-policy policy optimization
(
Xie and
Jiang 2020
)
.

We provide a discussion of and comparison to related work in
Section

9
. We offer some concluding remarks and directions for future research in
Section

10
.
All proofs are given in
Appendix

F
.

refers to the adjoint version of the completeness condition.

.

\alpha
on
d
-dimensional inputs. “First Order Efficiency” denotes whether the bound is first-order efficient.
“Convergence of
q,w
” denotes whether we can also derive estimation rates for the
q
- and
w
-functions themselves and the nature of the errors bounded (Bellman residual errors or L2-errors).

1.1
Notation

Functions and their expectations.

For a function
f:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
, we use
\mathbb{E}[f(s,a,r,s^{\prime})]
to denote the expectation with respect to
P_{S,A,R,S^{\prime}}(\cdot)
(the data generating distribution in
Eq.

2
) and
\mathbb{E}_{n}[f(s,a,r,s^{\prime})]
the empirical average over the
n
data points.
We set for any
f:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
and a policy
\pi(a|s)
, we define
f(s,\pi)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi(a|s)}[f(s,a)]
.
We also define
\|f\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{ess}\sup_{(s,a,r,s^{\prime})\sim P_{S,A,R,S^{\prime}}}|f(s,a,r,s^{\prime})|
,
\|f\|_{2}=(\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(s,a,r,s^{\prime})])^{1/2}
and
\|f\|_{2,n}=(\mathbb{E}_{n}[f^{2}(s,a,r,s^{\prime})])^{1/2}
.
For a set of functions
\mathcal{F}
, we define
\|\mathcal{F}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|f\|_{\infty}
and

\mathcal{F}+f:=\{f^{\prime}+f\mid f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}\}
.
For a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
,
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
is the space of square integrable functions over
\mathcal{X}
, where the inner product is defined as
\langle f,g\rangle=\mathbb{E}[fg]
for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
.
For a function
m:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and an operator
\mathcal{T}:f\mapsto\mathcal{T}f
,
m(\mathcal{F}):=\{m(f(\cdot)):f\in\mathcal{F}\}
and
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{F}:=\{\mathcal{T}f\mid f\in\mathcal{F}\}
denote sets of mapped functions.

Constants.

Symbols denoted by
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}\ldots
are universal positive constants that do not depend on any other parameters and can refer to different universal constants in
each
appearance, unless noted otherwise. In contrast,
C_{\eta},C_{w},\ldots
is reserved for non-universal, problem-dependent constants.

Comparisons.

For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
x\lesssim y
to mean
x\leq c_{0}y
.
For random variables
X
and
Y
, we write
X\lesssim_{\delta}Y
to mean
\mathbb{P}(X\leq c_{0}Y)\geq 1-\delta
holds for
any

\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, where
c_{0}
is independent of
\delta
.
For sequences
\{x_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
x_{n}=O(y_{n})
denotes that there exist
C>0
and
\bar{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|x_{n}|\leq C|y_{n}|
for all
n\geq\bar{n}
,

x_{n}=\widetilde{O}(y_{n})
denotes that there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|x_{n}|\leq C|y_{n}|\log^{k}(n)
,

x_{n}=\Omega(y_{n})
denotes
y_{n}=O(x_{n})
,

x_{n}=\Theta(y_{n})
denotes both
x_{n}=O(y_{n})
and
y_{n}=O(x_{n})
hold,
and similarly for
\widetilde{\Omega},\,\widetilde{\Theta}
.

Size of sets and matrices.

For a set
S
equipped with a norm
\|\cdot\|
and
\tau>0
, let
\mathcal{N}(\tau,S,\|\cdot\|)
denote the
\tau
-covering number of
S
with respect to
\|\cdot\|
,
i.e.
,
the minimal number of
\tau
-radius
\|\cdot\|
-balls needed to cover
S
. We say a set
S
is
star-shaped
if there exists
z_{0}\in S
such that
\forall z\in S,\forall\alpha\in[0,1],z_{0}\alpha+z(1-\alpha)\in S
.
For a matrix
A
, we denote the set of its all singular values of
A
by
\sigma(A)
, and
\sigma_{\max}(A)=\sup\sigma(A)
denotes its maximum.

Rademacher complexities and critical radii.

The empirical localized Rademacher complexity of a class
\mathcal{G}
of functions
V\to\mathbb{R}
for a given sample
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{n}(\eta;\mathcal{G})=\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}:\|g\|_{2,n}\leq\eta}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}g(v_{i})\right],

where
\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}
is shorthand for
\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}^{n}}
,
i.e.
, expectation over independent Rademacher variables (
i.e.
, not marginalizing any other randomness).
The empirical (non-localized) Rademacher complexity of
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G})=\mathcal{R}_{n}(\infty;\mathcal{G})
. A critical radius of
\mathcal{G}
is any solution
\eta
to
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\eta;\mathcal{G})\leq\eta^{2}/C_{2}
when
\|\mathcal{G}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{2}
.

2
Problem Setup

A (discounted) MDP is specified by
M=\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P_{S^{\prime}|S,A},P_{R|S,A},\gamma,d_{0}\}
as explained in
Section

1
.
Our goal is to estimate the normalized discounted return
J
under a given initial-state density
d_{0}
and the evaluation policy
\pi_{e}
defined in
Eq.

1
.
We remark that the policy value
J
can equivalently be written as

J=\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma},r\sim P_{R|S,A}(r|s,a)}[r]
,
where
d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma}
is the discounted occupancy density,
d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma}\coloneqq(1-\gamma)\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},t}
, and
d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},t}
is the density of
(s_{t},a_{t})
under policy
\pi^{\mathrm{e}}
and initial-state density
d_{0}
.

In OPE,
we must estimate
J
given data
generated in the same MDP by the behavior policy
\pi^{\mathrm{b}}
, namely,

n
independent and identically distributed quadruplets
\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s^{\prime}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim P_{S,A,R,S^{\prime}}(s,a,r,s^{\prime})
defined in
Eq.

2
.
With a slight abuse of notation, we refer to the joint density over
(s,a,r,s^{\prime})
or its marginal on
(s,a)
as
P_{S,A,R,S^{\prime}}(\cdot)
and
P_{S,A}(\cdot)
.

Remark 1
.

The OPE task is related to treatment effect estimation of dynamic treatment regimes in causal inference
(
Murphy 2003
;
Jiang
et al. 2019
;
Kosorok and
Moodie 2015
)
.
We can equivalently phrase our results in terms of counterfactual notation (e.g., potential outcomes) if we assume consistency and sequential ignorability
(
Ertefaie and
Strawderman 2018
;
Kennedy 2019
;
Luckett et al. 2018
;
Shi
et al. 2020
)
. Thus our work is also directly applicable to this setting.

We define the MDP’s transition and (forward) Bellman operators as, respectively,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}:[S\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}]\to[S\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}],~f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((s,a)\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|s,a)}[f(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})]\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:[S\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}]\to[S\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}],~f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((s,a)\mapsto\gamma\mathcal{T}f(s,a)+\mathbb{E}_{r\sim P_{R|S,A}(r|s,a)}[r]\right).

The (true)
q
-
function
is the long-term value of a given state-action pair under
\pi^{\mathrm{e}}
:

\displaystyle q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r_{t}|s_{0}=s,a_{0}=a\right],

where the expectation is taken with respect to
P_{R|S,A}(r_{0}\mid s_{0},a_{0})P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s_{1}|s_{0},a_{0})\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(a_{1}\mid s_{1})\cdots
.
The corresponding (true) state-value function is
v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s)=q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,\pi^{\mathrm{e}})
.

The (true)
w
-
function
is the density ratio of the state-action occupancy measures. In addition to the
w
-function, we also define its state-only analog as well as the instantaneous density ratio:

w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\coloneqq\frac{d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma}(s,a)}{P_{S,A}(s,a)},\qquad w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},S}(s)\coloneqq\frac{d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma}(s)}{P_{S}(s)},\qquad\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\coloneqq\frac{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(a\mid s)}{\pi^{\mathrm{b}}(a\mid s)},

where
d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma}(s)
is the marginal density of
d_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},\gamma}(s,a)
over
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

Based on
Eq.

3
and given estimators
\widehat{q}
and
\widehat{w}
for
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
and
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
, respectively, there are three general approaches to estimating
J
:
(i) the direct method (DM) given by
(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s\sim d_{0}}[\widehat{q}(s,\pi^{\mathrm{e}})]
, (ii) the marginal importance sampling (IS) estimator
(
Liu
et al. 2018
)
given by
\mathbb{E}_{n}[\widehat{w}(s,a)r]
, and (iii) the infinite-horizon doubly robust (DR) estimator
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
given by
(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s\sim d_{0}}[\widehat{q}(s,\pi^{\mathrm{e}})]+\mathbb{E}_{n}[\widehat{w}(s,a)(r-\widehat{q}(s,a)+\gamma\widehat{q}(s^{\prime},\pi_{e}))]
.

Remark 2
.

Our goal is to obtain finite sample results with polynomial dependence on problem-dependent quantities such as effective horizon. In contrast, IS or doubly robust estimators using sequential density ratios
\prod_{k=1}^{t}\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(a_{k}\mid s_{k})

(
Precup
et al. 2000
;
Jiang
et al. 2019b
;
Jiang and Li 2016
;
Thomas and
Brunskill 2016
)
have MSE growing
exponentially
in the effective horizon, which is known as curse of horizon.
Finite sample results that avoid this dependence have been largely limited to the DM method based on FQI
(
Ernst
et al. 2005
)
, where
with
q
-realizability (
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}
) and
q
-completeness (
\mathcal{B}\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathbb{Q}
) good finite-sample results are obtained
(
Munos and
Szepesvári 2008
;
Chen and
Jiang 2019
)
.

We assume throughout that
P_{R|S,A}
is supported only on the bounded interval
[-R_{\max},R_{\max}]
. We further assume throughout the following

Assumption 1

(Concentrability)
.

Letting
P_{S^{\prime}}(\cdot)
denote the density of
s^{\prime}
under
P_{S,A,R,S^{\prime}}(\cdot)
, there exist
C_{\eta},C_{m},C_{w}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\eta},\quad\|P_{S^{\prime}}(\cdot)/P_{S}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{m},\quad\|w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{w}=C_{\eta}C_{m}.

These assumptions are standard in offline RL
(
Antos
et al. 2008
)
and ensure the data has sufficient exploration to identify
J
.
In the non-dynamic setting (
\gamma=0
) and in causal inference, it is also called the overlap assumption
(
Kennedy 2019
)
.
Note that if
P_{S}(\cdot)
is invariant (stationary) under the Markov kernel
\pi_{b}(a\mid s)P_{S^{\prime}\mid S,A}(\cdot\mid s,a)
then
P_{S}(\cdot)=P_{S^{\prime}}(\cdot)
and we can take
C_{m}=1
.

Under
1
,
\mathcal{T}
is a bounded operator.
Thus, there uniquely exists the
adjoint
transition operator
(
Debnath 2005
)
, as summarized below (
1
is assumed implicitly everywhere).

Lemma 2.1
.

The adjoint transition operator
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is uniquely defined such that

\displaystyle\langle f,\mathcal{T}g\rangle=\langle\mathcal{T}^{\prime}f,g\rangle,\,\forall f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}).

In
Appendix

B
, we show the explicit form of the adjoint transition operator and how it can be interpreted in specific MDPs such as tabular MDPs and linear MDPs
(
Jin
et al. 2020
)
.

We also define the
backward
Bellman operator:

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:~f\mapsto\left((s,a)\mapsto\gamma\mathcal{T}^{\prime}f(s,a)+(1-\gamma)\frac{d_{0}(s,a)\pi^{\mathrm{e}}(a|s)}{P_{S,A}(s,a)}\right).

In RL,
\mathcal{T},\mathcal{B}
are natural for describing
q
-functions.

\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
can be understood as the analogues for
w
-functions. Then next lemma establishes the
q
- and
w
-Bellman equations.

Lemma 2.2
.

By letting
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\coloneqq\gamma\mathcal{T}-I,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\coloneqq\gamma\mathcal{T}^{\prime}-I
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}-I)q=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\;\forall q,\qquad(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}-I)w=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\;\forall w.

In particular, we have
\mathcal{B}q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}=q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},\,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}=w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
.

The Bellman residual error of any
q
is defined as
\|\mathcal{B}q-q\|_{2}
. This is a common error objective in RL
(
Chen and
Jiang 2019
)
since it is zero for
q=q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
.
We introduce an analogous notion for the
w
-function by defining Bellman residual error of any
w
as
\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}w-w\|_{2}
. This is again
0
when
w=w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
.
In contrast, L2-estimation error refers to
\|q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-q\|_{2}
or
\|w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-w\|_{2}
.

Generally, Bellman residual errors and L2-errors are not equivalent, unless
\gamma
is small.

Lemma 2.3
.

We have
(1-\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}})\|q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathcal{B}q-q\|_{2}\leq(1+\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}})\|q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|
and
(1-\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}})\|w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}w-w\|_{2}\leq(1+\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}})\|w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}
.
Thus, when
\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}}<1
, Bellman residual errors and L2-errors are equivalent.

3
Minimax Estimators for
q
-functions and
w
-functions

We next define the minimax estimators for
q
-functions and
w
-functions in a generic, unified way.
The estimators take a form of minimax games, unlike standard empirical risk minimization.
To introduce the estimators, we observe that the following moment conditions
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
characterizing
w
- and
q
-functions hold: for any functions
w,q\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})
, the true functions
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
and
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
satisfy

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[w(s,a)\{r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+\gamma q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}],

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\{-q(s,a)+\gamma v(s^{\prime})\}]+(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0}}[v(s_{0})],\,v(s)=q(s,\pi^{\mathrm{e}}).

These two equations are derived by the Bellman equations of
q
-functions and Bellman flow constraints of state-action occupancy measures.
Note that the first condition is also formulated as a conditional moment equation:
\mathbb{E}[r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+\gamma q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\mid s,a]=0
. From the above moment conditions, we have

\displaystyle w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{w\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}\max_{q\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}|\mathbb{E}[w(s,a)\{-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]+(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0}}[q(s_{0},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})]|,

\displaystyle q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}

\displaystyle\in\argmin_{q\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}\max_{w\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A})}|\mathbb{E}[w(s,a)\{r-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]|.

Motivated
1
1

1

Note we take this purely as motivation as the equations need not uniquely define
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
; see
Example

1
. Later, we will prove specific guarantees for our specific estimators under appropriate conditions.
by this,
by introducing function classes
\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathbb{Q}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}
and possible stabilization coefficients
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\geq 0
and by replacing
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
with the empirical analogue
\mathbb{E}_{n}[\cdot]
, we define the minimax indirect learning (MIL) estimators:

\displaystyle\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{1}}\max_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{n}[w(s,a)\{-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]+(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0}}[q(s_{0},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})]-\lambda\|\mathcal{J}q\|^{2}_{2,n},

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}}\max_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{n}[w(s,a)\{r-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]-\lambda^{\prime}\|w\|^{2}_{2,n},

where
\mathcal{J}q=-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})
.
Intuitively,
\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{W}_{2}
are hypothesis classes for
w_{\pi}^{\mathrm{e}}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}
for
q_{\pi}^{\mathrm{e}}
. When there is no confusion, we often write
\mathbb{W}
for
\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{W}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Q}
for
\mathbb{Q}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. We let
C_{\mathbb{Q}},C_{\mathbb{W}}
be some constants such that
\|q\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\mathbb{Q}},\forall q\in\mathbb{Q},\|w\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\mathbb{W}},\forall w\in\mathbb{W}
. For simplicity, we assume these function classes are symmetric (
\mathbb{W}_{1}=-\mathbb{W}_{1}
, etc.).

Given these estimators for
w_{\pi}^{\mathrm{e}}
and
q_{\pi}^{\mathrm{e}}
, we consider the resulting DM (
q
-based), IS (
w
-based), and DR (
q,w
-based) estimators for
J
based on
\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}},\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0}}[\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}(s_{0},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})],\quad\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w}=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}(s,a)r],

\displaystyle\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}(s,a)\{r-\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}(s,a)+\gamma\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]+(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0}}[\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}(s_{0},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})].

These minimax estimators are inspired by and in some sense unify the various proposals in
(
Antos
et al. 2008
;
Chen and
Jiang 2019
;
Uehara
et al. 2020
;
Zhang
et al. 2020
;
Nachum
et al. 2019
;
Feng
et al. 2019
;
Tang
et al. 2020
;
Liao
et al. 2020
)
. When
\lambda=0.5
,
\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}
is modified BRM (Bellman Residual Minimization)
(
Antos
et al. 2008
;
Liao
et al. 2020
;
Chen and
Jiang 2019
)
. When
\lambda=0
,
\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}
is MWL in
(
Uehara
et al. 2020
)
. When
\lambda^{\prime}=0
,
\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}
is MQL in
(
Uehara
et al. 2020
)
. Note that, however, this is different from
(
Yang
et al. 2020
)
as in their formulation
\lambda\|\mathcal{J}q\|^{2}_{2,n}
is replaced by
\lambda\|q(s,a)\|^{2}_{2,n}
, where
\lambda=1
reduces to GenDICE
(
Zhang
et al. 2020
)
. Though
(
Yang
et al. 2020
)
uses more general norm rather than
L^{2}
-norm for stabilizers, we can also extend our analysis to this case. At any rate,
(
Yang
et al. 2020
)
does not provide any finite-sample guarantees. There are also several related estimators have been proposed in the non-dynamic setting (
\gamma=0
)
(
Hirshberg and
Wager 2019
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Wong and
Chan 2018
;
Armstrong and
Kolesár 2017
;
Chernozhukov
et al. 2018
)
.

4
Finite Sample Analysis Without Stabilizers under
w
- and
q
-realizability

In this section we analyze the MIL estimators without stabilizers (
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}=0
throughout this section).

First, we consider the case where we assume that
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{Q}
each contain the true
w
- and
q
-functions, respectively, which we call
w
- and
q
-realizability. Here, we do
not
assume any completeness assumptions.
The below extends
(
Uehara
et al. 2020
, Theorem 9)
to
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}
and empirical complexities. More crucially, going beyond
(
Uehara
et al. 2020
)
, we will proceed to analyze the error terms to also obtain a rate.

Theorem 4.1

(Finite sample error bound of MIL)
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{w(s,a)(-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})):w\in\mathbb{W},\,q\in\mathbb{Q}\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{1}))+C_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}(R_{\max}+C_{\mathbb{Q}_{1}})\sqrt{\log(c_{0}/\delta)n^{-1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{mil},w}

\displaystyle\coloneqq R_{\max}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathbb{W}_{1})+R_{\max}C_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}\sqrt{\log(c_{0}/\delta)n^{-1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q}

\displaystyle\coloneqq R_{\max}\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathbb{W}_{2})+\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathbb{Q}_{2}))+C_{\mathbb{W}_{2}}(R_{\max}+C_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}})\sqrt{\log(c_{0}/\delta)n^{-1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},wq}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}))+C_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}(R_{\max}+C_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}})\sqrt{\log(c_{0}/\delta)n^{-1}}.

Then:

1.

If
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{1}
, then
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w}+\mathrm{Err}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{mil},w}
.

2.

If
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, then
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q}
.

3.

If either
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\subset\mathbb{Q}_{1}
or
\mathbb{W}_{1}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\subset\mathbb{W}_{2},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, then
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\max(\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w},\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q})+\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},wq}
.

We remark on the implications. First, the dominating term with respect to
n
in all of the error terms is
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{W}_{i},\mathbb{Q}_{j})),i,j=1,2
. In other words, When
\mathbb{W}=\mathbb{W}_{1}=\mathbb{W}_{2}
,
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\mathbb{Q}_{2}
,
\mathcal{R}_{n}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}))
is an essential term in all of the bounds. We will analyze this term in detail soon when we consider complexity assumptions on
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}
.
Second, comparing to
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w}
and
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}
, the estimator
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}
requires an additional condition, that
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
or
\mathbb{W}_{2}
is large enough in addition to realizability.
Third,
\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}},\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}
might not actually converge to
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
, and irrespective of that, we can still ensure that the MIL OPE estimators converge. This is observed in the following example.

Example 1
.

Consider
\mathbb{W}_{2}=\{w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\},\mathbb{Q}_{2}=\{\alpha_{1}q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}+\alpha_{2}:\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R},\,\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Note that

\displaystyle 0=\mathbb{E}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\{r-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]

for any
q(s,a)=\alpha_{1}q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+\alpha_{2}
such that
(\gamma-1)\alpha_{2}=\mathbb{E}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)r(1-\alpha_{1})]
. Therefore,
\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}
may converge to any such
q
(or not converge at all), and not necessarily to
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
. This is intuitively because the discriminator class
\mathbb{W}_{2}
is not rich enough to identify
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
since
\mathbb{W}_{2}
is smaller than
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. Irrespective of that, we can still ensure
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}=J+\mathrm{o}_{p}(1)
since the realizability conditions
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
hold as in
Item

2
in
Theorem

4.1
.

Finally, we remark that we can also obtain an extension of
Theorem

4.1
agnostic to realizability assumptions, which is useful to analyze sieve estimators. In this case, the final error is given as the estimation error terms in
Theorem

4.1
plus an approximation error coming from the violation of realizability. For instance, the approximation error term for
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}
is

\displaystyle\min_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{2}}\max_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}}\mathbb{E}[(w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})]+\min_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}}\max_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{2}}\mathbb{E}[w\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})].

(4)

The first term corresponds to the violation of
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
and the second term corresponds to the violation of
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. We formalize the above extension and also discuss the approximation error terms of other estimators in
Appendix

C
.

4.1
Analysis of the error term

We next investigate the error terms in
Theorem

4.1
.
To simplify the presentation, we focus on the order with respect to the sample size.
In particular, in the below, all constants in
O(\cdot)
notation have
polynomial
dependence on both
\log(1/\delta)
and all problem-dependent quantities such as the effective horizon (
1/(1-\gamma)
) and the concentrability constants.
Below,
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}
refers to any one of
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w},\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q},\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}
assuming the conditions in the corresponding item in
Theorem

4.1
.
We let
\mathbb{W}_{1}=\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\mathbb{Q}_{2}=\mathbb{Q}
.

Example 2

(VC-subgraph classes)
.

Let
V(\mathbb{W}),V(\mathbb{Q})
be the VC-subgraph dimension (pseudodimension) of
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}
, respectively
(
van der
Vaart 1998
, Chapter 19)
. For example,
\mathbb{W}=\{(s,a)\mapsto\theta^{\top}\phi(s,a):\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
has VC-subgraph dimension
d+1
.

Corollarry 4.2
.

Suppose
V(\mathbb{W})
and
V(\mathbb{Q})
are finite. Then,
with probability
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}-J|=O(\sqrt{\max(V(\mathbb{W}),V(\mathbb{Q}))/n})

Example 3

(Nonparametric Models)
.

To study a case where
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are nonparametric, we can consider their covering numbers.
This covers examples such as function classes given by balls in Hölder, Sobolev, or Besov space
(
Korostelev and
Korosteleva 2011
)
, which do not have VC-subgraph dimension.
E.g.
, when
\mathbb{W}
is a ball with radius
c
in the Hölder space with smoothness
p
and input dimension
d
, we have
\log\mathcal{N}(\tau,\mathbb{W},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O((c/\tau)^{d/p})

(
Wainwright 2019
, Chapter 5)
.

Corollarry 4.3
.

Suppose
\log\mathcal{N}(\tau,\mathbb{W},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O(1/\tau^{\beta}),\log\mathcal{N}(\tau,\mathbb{Q},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O(1/\tau^{\beta})
for
some
\beta>0
. Then, with probability
1-\delta
,

|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}-J|=O(n^{-1/(2\vee\beta)})
if
\beta\neq 2
and
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}-J|=\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
if
\beta=2
.

Example 4

(Linear Sieves)
.

In practice, it is difficult to implement MIL with nonparametric classes as in
Example

3
because it involves an infinite-dimensional optimization.
Instead, we can achieve similar results by restricting to a function class that is linear in some basis functions, such as polynomials, splines, or wavelets
(
Chen 2007
, Section 2.3)
, and then slowly grow the number of such functions, aka a linear sieve.

We focus on
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}=[0,1]^{d}

and the target function class being
\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
, the ball of radius
c
in the Hölder space with order
p
and
d
-dimensional input.
Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\dots
be given bases functions such that,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{f\in\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})}\inf_{g\in\operatorname{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k})}\|f-g\|_{\infty}=O(k^{-p/d}).

(5)

This can, for example, be satisfied by the above mentioned basis functions
(
Chen 2007
, Section 2.3)
.
Then, for an appropriate choice
k_{n}
, we set
\mathbb{Q}=(-(1-\gamma)^{-1}R_{\max})\vee((1-\gamma)^{-1}R_{\max}\wedge\operatorname{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k_{n}}))
and
\mathbb{W}=(-C_{w})\vee(C_{w}\wedge\operatorname{span}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k_{n}}))
.

Since the function classes
\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{W}
change with
n
, it is not suitable to directly assume
w
,
q
-realizability , as we did Corollary
4.2
and
4.3
. Instead, we assume realizability  with respect to
\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
, which
\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{W}
approximate, and instead of applying
Theorem

4.1
directly, we will need to also incorporate an approximation error as in
Eq.

4
.

Corollarry 4.4
.

Suppose (
5
) holds. Assume
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
. Then, for an appropriate choice of
k_{n}
, with probability
1-\delta
,
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}-J|=\widetilde{O}(n^{-{p}/({2p+d})})
.

This rate can be worse than the rate when we directly optimize over
\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
, in which case the rate is
1/n^{\frac{1}{2}\wedge\frac{p}{d}}
(Corollary
4.3
).

Example 5

(Neural Networks)
.

Neural networks can also form a (nonlinear) sieve for nonparametric classes
(
Yarotsky 2017
;
Nakada and
Imaizumi 2020
)
.
We consider neutral networks with a ReLU activation function, which can approximate functions in
H^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
, the
K
-ball in the Sobolev space with an order
p
on
d
-dimensional input
(
Korostelev and
Korosteleva 2011
)
.
Again we focus on
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}=[0,1]^{d}
and assume
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in H^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
instead of the realizability  of
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}
. We then have the following:

Corollarry 4.5
.

Assume
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in H^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
.
Let
\mathbb{W}=\mathcal{F}_{NN}=\mathbb{Q}=\mathcal{F}_{NN}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{NN}
is the set of functions given by neural networks with ReLU activation,
L
layers, and
\Xi
weight parameters in
[-B,B]
. Then, by taking
L=\Theta(\log n),\,\Xi=\Theta(n^{d/(2p+d)})
, with probability
1-\delta
,
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}-J|=\widetilde{O}(n^{-{p}/({2p+d})})
.

The rate in Corollary
4.5
is derived by balancing the approximation (bias) and estimation errors (Rademacher complexities).
The above analysis does not necessarily capture practical situations. Usually the number of parameters
\Xi
is taken much larger than the sample size
n
so that the bias is very small.
To take this into consideration, we discuss an overparameterized case, where instead of limiting the number of weight parameters,
we focus on the the norm of the weight parameter matrix.
We then obtain the following result by combining an approximation rate of neural networks
(
Yarotsky 2017
)
and the parameter norm bound
(
Golowich
et al. 2018
)
.

Corollarry 4.6
.

Fix
M_{F}(\ell)\geq 0,\,\ell=1,\dots,L
and
let
\mathbb{W}=\mathcal{F}_{NN}^{\circ},\mathbb{Q}=\mathcal{F}_{NN}^{\circ}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{NN}^{\circ}
is the set of neural network with
L
layers and ReLU activation function subject to
\|A_{\ell}\|_{F}\leq M_{F}(\ell),\,\ell=1,\dots,L
, where
A_{\ell}
is the weight matrix of the
\ell
-th layer.
Suppose that
\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
is compact and that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
w_{0}\in\mathbb{W},q_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
\max\{\|w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-w_{0}\|_{\infty},\|q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-q_{0}\|_{\infty}\}\leq\varepsilon
.
Then, with probability
1-\delta
,
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}-J|=\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon+\sqrt{\log L}\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}M_{F}(\ell)/\sqrt{n})
.

Here,
\varepsilon
represents the approximation error of neural networks. The approximation error is guaranteed to be small enough due to the large number of parameters. The final error only depends on the parameter norm bound
M_{F}(\ell)
but not the number of parameters. Comparing to Corollary
4.5
, which uses
o(n)
parameters, Corollary
4.6
captures the case in which the number of parameters can be much larger than the sample size.

5
Finite Sample Analysis Without Stabilizers under
w
- and
q
-completeness

In this section, we analyze MIL estimators without stabilizers (again,
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}=0
throughout this section) where we relax
w
- or
q
-realizability. Instead, we introduce completeness conditions and show they are sufficient to obtain convergence rates.
All of the conditions are new aside from the (non-adjoint)
q
-completeness condition, which is closely related to the standard completeness condition in FQI as discussed below.

Theorem 5.1

(Convergence rates in MIL)
.

For a problem-dependent constant
C_{\xi}
,

1.

If
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1},C_{\xi}(\mathbb{W}_{1}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\mathbb{Q}_{1}
then
\|\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}\sqrt{C^{-1}_{\xi}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w}}
,

2.

If
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1},C_{\xi}\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{W}_{1}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{Q}_{1}
then
\|\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}\sqrt{C^{-1}_{\xi}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w}}
,

3.

If

q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2},C_{\xi}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{2}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
then
\|\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}-\mathcal{B}\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}\sqrt{C^{-1}_{\xi}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q}}
,

4.

If

q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2},C_{\xi}(\mathbb{Q}_{2}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\mathbb{W}_{2}
, then
\|\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}\sqrt{C^{-1}_{\xi}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q}}
,

where
\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},*}
is as defined in
Theorem

4.1
.

Note that, whereas
Theorem

4.1
provided bounds directly on OPE (and
\widehat{q}^{\mathrm{mil}},\widehat{w}^{\mathrm{mil}}
may well be inconsistent),
Theorem

5.1
provides convergence rates for
q
and
w
themselves.
The
\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},*}
terms can be controlled exactly as done in
Section

4.1
. Note, however, that these terms appear in a square root in the bounds. In this sense, the rates are
slow
, for example of order
O(n^{-1/4})
for VC-subgraph classes.
We discuss the meaning of the assumptions in the next two subsections.
We discuss the implications for OPE in
Section

5.3
.
This will also correspond to slow rates for OPE as we will explain.
We will obtain
fast
rates in
Section

6
by introducing stabilizers.

5.1
q
-completeness and adjoint
q
-completeness

First, we discuss the conditions in
Items

3
and
4
in
Theorem

5.1
.
By Lemma
2.2
, the condition in
Item

3
is equivalent to
C_{\xi}(\mathcal{B}-I)\mathbb{Q}_{2}\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
.
When
\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is convex symmetric, this condition is immediately implied by
\mathcal{B}\mathbb{Q}_{2}\subset\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, which is the usual completeness condition of FQI
(
Munos and
Szepesvári 2008
;
Chen and
Jiang 2019
)
. Because of this closeness, we refer to this condition as
q
-completeness, but our condition may be very slightly weaker in general.
In linear MDPs
(
Jin
et al. 2020
)
where the reward and transition densities are linear, it is known that this condition holds when
\mathbb{Q}
is linear functions
(
Agarwal
et al. 2019
, Chapter 15)
(see also I, II in Lemma
5.2
below).

The condition in
Item

4
in
Theorem

5.1
is a flipped version of
q
-completeness, which we call adjoint
q
-completeness. This condition also holds under some additional assumptions, as we show below (Item III in Lemma
5.2
). In particular, in the tabular case, we can use unrestricted classes
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}
and
q
-completeness and adjoint
q
-completeness hold (Item IV in Lemma
5.2
). This is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

For
\phi:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, suppose
P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|s,a)=\vartheta(s^{\prime})^{\top}\phi(s,a)
,
\mathbb{E}_{r\sim P_{R|S,A}}[r\mid s,a]=\theta^{\top}\phi(s,a)
for some
\vartheta:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mathbb{Q}\coloneqq\{(s,a)\mapsto\beta^{\top}\phi(s,a):\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. Then:
(I)
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}
; (II)
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{Q}
; (III) for
M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
satisfying
\mathcal{T}(\beta^{\top}\phi)=(M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\beta)^{\top}\phi
(which always exists), if
1/\gamma\notin\sigma(M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})
and
\mathbb{E}[\phi(s,a)\phi^{\top}(s,a)]
is non-singular then
(\mathbb{Q}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\mathbb{Q}
; (IV) if the state and action spaces are finite,
P_{S,A}(s,a)>0,\,\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, and
\phi
is the standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{Q},\,(\mathbb{Q}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\mathbb{Q}
.

The condition
1/\gamma\notin\sigma(M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})
in III in Lemma
5.2
is satisfied, when we can guarantee
M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
is a stochastic matrix, i.e., its entries are positive and rows add up to
1

(
Barreto
et al. 2014
)
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
\mathbf{1}^{\top}\phi(s,\pi^{\mathrm{e}})=1\,\forall s\in\mathcal{S},\int\vartheta(s)\mathrm{d}\mu(s)=\mathbf{1}
, where
\mathbf{1}
is a
d
-dimensional vector whose element is
1
. Then,
M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
is a stochatic matrix. Thus,
\forall b\in\sigma(M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})
,
|b|\leq 1
.

Lemmas
5.2
and
5.3
together ensure adjoint
q
-completeness holds (
(\mathbb{Q}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\mathbb{Q}
).
Finally, we remark that when
P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|\cdot)\,P_{R|S,A}(r|\cdot)
belong to the Hölder or Sobolev space, a similar argument shows
q
-completeness holds when
\mathbb{W}_{2},\,\mathbb{Q}_{2}
are balls in that space.

5.2
w
-completeness and adjoint
w
-completeness

Next, we discuss the conditions in
Items

2
and
1
in
Theorem

5.1
.
By Lemma
2.2
, the condition in
Item

2
is equivalent to
C_{\xi}(\mathcal{B}-I)\mathbb{W}_{1}\subset\mathbb{Q}_{1}
.
We refer to this as
w
-completeness .
We refer as adjoint
w
-completeness to its flipped version in
Item

1
. Note
w
-completeness and adjoint
w
-completeness do not imply each other, and each condition leads to a different convergence guarantee.
We can again guarantee that both
w
-completeness and adjoint
w
-completeness hold in many settings and in particular in the tabular case.

Lemma 5.4
.

For
\psi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, suppose
P_{S,A|S^{\prime}}(s,a|s^{\prime})=\vartheta(s,a)^{\top}\psi(s^{\prime})
,
d_{0}(s)/P_{S}(s)=\theta^{\top}\psi(s)
for some
\vartheta:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
P_{S,A|S^{\prime}}(s,a|s^{\prime})
is the posterior density of
(s,a)
given
s^{\prime}
. Assume
P_{S^{\prime}}=P_{S}
, i.e., the data come from a stationary distribution associated with
\pi_{b}
.
Let
\mathbb{W}\coloneqq\{(s,a)\mapsto\beta^{\top}\psi(s)\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a):\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}.

Then:
(I)
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}
;
(II)
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{W}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{W}
;
(III) for
M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
satisfying
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\beta^{\top}\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\psi)=(M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\beta)^{\top}\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\psi
(which always exists), if
1/\gamma\notin\sigma(M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})
and
\mathbb{E}[\psi(s)\psi^{\top}(s)]
is non-singular then
(\mathbb{W}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\mathbb{W}
,
(IV)
If the state and action spaces are finite,
P_{S,A}(s,a)>0\,\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
,
and
\psi(s,a)
is the standard basis in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
, then
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{W}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{W},\,(\mathbb{W}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\mathbb{W}
.

The stationarity assumption in Lemma
5.4
is reasonable if the data is generated by long trajectories. Notice that compared to a linear MDP, here the linearity assumption is agnostic to the reward density and is imposed on the
posterior
transition density
P_{S,A|S^{\prime}}(s,a\mid s^{\prime})
rather than the transition density
P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|s,a)
.
Again, when the feature vector is stochastic, we will have
\sigma_{\max}(M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\leq 1
and satisfy (III) in Lemma
5.4
. Concretely, let
\mathbf{1}
be a
d
-dimensional vector. Assume

\mathbf{1}^{\top}\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\psi(s)=1\,\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A},\int\vartheta(s,a)\mathrm{d}\mu(s,a)=\mathbf{1}.

Then,
M^{\prime\top}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
is a stochastic matrix. This implies for any
b\in\sigma(M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})
,
|b|\leq 1

(
Meyer 2000
)
.

5.3
Application to Policy Value Estimation

We now show how
Theorem

5.1
can be translated into an error bound for OPE.

Corollarry 5.5
.

For a problem-dependent constant
C_{\xi}
,

1.

If
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1}
and either
C_{\xi}\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{W}_{1}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{Q}_{1}
or
C_{\xi}(\mathbb{W}_{1}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}\mathbb{Q}_{1}
, then
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\mathrm{Err}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{mil},w}+C_{\mathbb{Q}}\sqrt{C^{-1}_{\xi}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w}}
.

2.

If
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
and either
C_{\xi}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{2}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
or
C_{\xi}(\mathbb{Q}_{2}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\mathbb{W}_{2}
, then
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}C_{\mathbb{W}}\sqrt{C^{-1}_{\xi}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q}}
.

3.

If any one of the four conditions in Theorem
5.1
hold,
then

|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},wq}+C^{-1/2}_{\xi}\max(\penalty C_{\mathbb{Q}}\sqrt{\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},w}},\,C_{\mathbb{W}}\sqrt{\mathrm{Err}_{\mathrm{mil},q}}).

While the conditions do not require both
w
-realizability and
q
-realizability, the rates in Corollary
5.5
are generally slower than the ones of
Theorem

4.1
. For example, for VC-subgraph classes, the order in Corollary
5.5
is
O(n^{-1/4})
and the order in
Theorem

4.1
is
O(n^{-1/2})
.
Item

3
in Corollary
5.5
is a double robustness result: it says that the estimator is consistent as long as
w
-realizability and some
w
-completeness or
q
-realizability and some
q
-completeness holds.

6
Finite Sample Analysis With Stabilizers under
w
- and
q
-completeness

In this section, we show MIL with stabilizers (
\lambda>0,\lambda^{\prime}>0
) attains
fast
rates under completeness. This gives the
first
result for a fast rate of estimating
w
-functions in a general function approximation setting. For
q
-functions, this allows us to obtain fast rates using MIL of the same order as FQI (see
Appendix

E
regarding FQI).
We then study implications for OPE and compare to results without stabilizers.

6.1
Fast Rates for
w
- and
q
-function Estimation

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}_{w,n}
be an upper bound on the critical radii of
\mathcal{G}_{w1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{w2}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{w1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(s,a,s^{\prime})\mapsto(q(s,a)-\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})):q\in\mathbb{Q}_{1}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{w2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(s,a,s^{\prime})\mapsto(w(s,a)-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a))(-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})):w\in\mathbb{W}_{1},q\in\mathbb{Q}_{1}\}.

Suppose
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1}
,
C_{\xi}\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{W}_{1}-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{Q}_{1}
(i.e.,
C_{\xi}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}-I)\mathbb{W}_{1}\subset\mathbb{Q}_{1}
), and that
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
is star shaped.
Then,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}-\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil}}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}C^{*}_{w,n}\eta_{w,n},

\displaystyle\textstyle C^{*}_{w,n}=1+C_{m}C_{\eta}+\left(\lambda^{2}+C^{2}_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}\right)/\lambda+C_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}+\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}}C_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}+{C^{-1}_{\xi}},

\displaystyle\textstyle\eta_{w,n}=\eta^{\prime}_{w,n}c_{0}\sqrt{{\log(c_{1}/\delta)}/{n}}.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\eta^{\prime}_{q,n}
be an upper bound on the critical radii of
\mathbb{W}_{2}
and
\mathcal{G}_{q}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{q}:=\{(s,a,s^{\prime})\mapsto(-q(s,a)+q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})-\gamma q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}}))w(s,a):q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2},w\in\mathbb{W}_{2}\}.

Suppose
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
,
C_{\xi}\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{2}-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
(i.e.,
C_{\xi}(\mathcal{B}-I)\mathbb{Q}_{2}\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
), and that
\mathbb{W}_{2}
is star shaped. Then,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}-\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\delta}C^{*}_{q,n}\eta_{q,n},

\displaystyle\textstyle C^{*}_{q,n}=1+R_{\max}+C_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}+\left(\lambda^{\prime 2}+(R_{\max}+C_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}})^{2}\right)/{\lambda^{\prime}}+{C^{-1}_{\xi}},

\displaystyle\textstyle\eta_{q,n}=\eta^{\prime}_{q,n}c_{0}\sqrt{{\log(c_{1}/\delta)}/{n}}.

These theorems state that MIL with stabilizers converge at fast rates when we assume realizability and completeness. Critical radii are commonly used to tightly derive the convergence rate of regression with general function approximation
(
Wainwright 2019
)
. For example, when we use VC-subgrah classes (parametric models), we can obtain a rate of
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
from
Theorems

6.2
and
6.1
as we will see soon. Recall that the rate in
Section

5
without stabilizers is
O(n^{-1/4})
and the primary required assumptions are the same, i.e., realizability and completeness. The improved rate is owing to a localization technique based on localized Rademacher complexity
(
Bartlett
et al. 2005
)
. We emphasize the required analysis in our setting is significantly more challenging than in the standard nonparametric regression setting.

In
Theorems

6.2
and
6.1
, we bound Bellman residual errors under realizability and completeness conditions .
Like
Theorem

5.1
, similar bounds can be obtained for L2-errors
under corresponding realizability and
adjoint
completeness conditions, which we give in
Appendix

D
.

We can also obtain bounds agnostic to realizability and completeness assumptions instead with some bias terms accounting for the approximation.
For example, for
w
- or
q
-function estimation, we have the additional bias terms, respectively

\displaystyle\min_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{1}}\|\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-w)\|_{2}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\max_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{1}}\min_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{1}}\|q-\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\gamma}(w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\|_{2},\quad\text{or}

(6)

\displaystyle\min_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}}\|\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-q)\|_{2}\qquad

and

\displaystyle\max_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}}\min_{w\in\mathbb{W}_{2}}\|w-\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}(q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})\|_{2}.

(7)

The first term in each of
Eqs.

6
and
7
corresponds to violations of realizability and the second to violations of completeness .
Detail is given in
Appendix

C
.
We use this more general result for analyzing sieve estimators.

6.2
Analysis of critical radius

We next analyze
\eta_{q,n}
for several function classes, focusing on the rate with respect to
n
. In particular, all constant in
O(\cdot)
notation below have
polynomial
dependence on both
\log(1/\delta)
and all problem-dependent quantities.
In each case we study, the rate of
\eta_{w,n}
can analogously be calculated.

Example 6

(VC-subgraph classes)
.

Assume
V(\mathbb{W}_{2}),V(\mathbb{Q}_{2})
are finite.

Corollarry 6.3
.

With probability
1-\delta
,

\eta_{q,n}=O(\sqrt{\max(V(\mathbb{W}_{2}),V(\mathbb{Q}_{2}))\log n/n}).

Example 7

(Nonparametric Models)
.

As in
Example

3
, we consider nonparametric models that are characterized by covering numbers.

Corollarry 6.4
.

Assume
\log\mathcal{N}(\tau,\mathbb{W}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O(1/\tau^{\beta}),\log\mathcal{N}(\tau,\mathbb{Q}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})=O(1/\tau^{\beta})
for some
\beta>0
. Then,
with probability
1-\delta
,
\eta_{q,n}=\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/((2+\beta)\vee(2\beta))})
.

To gain intuition, compare to the much simpler problem of nonparametric regression, in which case the minimax optimal rate for L2-error for a function class with covering entropy
O(1/\tau^{\beta})
is
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/(2+\beta)})

(
Wainwright 2019
, Chapter 15)
. We match this rate for
\beta\leq 2
.
For
\beta>2
we can typically improve the rate with a more analysis depending on the particular function class.

Example 8

(Linear Sieves)
.

We consider linear sieves
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}
as in
Example

4
.

Corollarry 6.5
.

Suppose (
5
) holds.
Assume
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
,
P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|\cdot)\in\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
, and
2\mathbb{Q}_{2}+\mathbb{W}^{\prime}_{2}\subset\mathbb{W}_{2}
.
Then, for an appropriate choice of
k_{n}
,
with probability
1-\delta
,
\|\mathcal{B}\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}-\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}\|_{2}=\widetilde{O}(n^{-p/(2p+d)})
.

Since the function classes
\mathbb{Q}_{2},\mathbb{W}_{2}
grow with
n
, it is not suitable to directly assume realizability and completeness on
\mathbb{Q}_{2},\mathbb{W}_{2}
. We instead assume sufficient conditions that ensure realizability and completeness on the Hölder balls and leverage a sieve approximation and an extension of
Theorem

6.2
given in
Appendix

C
that accounts for the approximation errors.
In particular,
P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|\cdot)\in\Lambda^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
is sufficient to ensure completeness of Hölder balls.
Notice that when
d>2p
, the rate here is faster than what would be implied by Corollary
6.4
for Hölder balls (which have a covering entropy rate of
\beta=d/p
).

Example 9

(Neural Networks)
.

As in
Example

5
, we consider using neural networks as sieves. Like the above example, we impose the realizability and completeness on the ball
H^{p}_{K}([0,1]^{d})
in the Hölder space. Then, we have the following result.

Corollarry 6.6
.

Assume
q_{\pi^{e}}\in H_{K}^{p}([0,1]^{d})
and
P_{S^{\prime}|S,A}(s^{\prime}|\cdot)\in H_{K}^{p}([0,1]^{d})
.
Let
\mathbb{Q}_{2}=\mathcal{F}_{NN}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{NN}
is the set of functions given by neural networks with ReLU activation,
L
layers, and
\Xi
weight parameters in
[-B,B]
.
Let
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}_{2}
be the same set and define
\mathbb{W}_{2}
as the
(L+1)
-layer networks given by
\mathbb{W}_{2}:=\mathcal{F}_{NN}+\mathcal{F}_{NN}+\mathcal{F}_{NN}
.
Then, by taking
L=\Theta(\log n),\Xi=\Theta(n^{d/(2p+d)})
, with probability
1-\delta
, we have

\|\mathcal{B}\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}-\widehat{q}_{\mathrm{mil}}\|_{2}=\widetilde{O}(n^{-p/(2p+d)})

As with
Example

5
, we can again extend the result to the overparametrized regime. Here, we can do so by noting that the squared critical radius is always upper-bounded by the (non-localized) Rademacher complexity, which is calculated in Corollary
4.6
.

6.3
Application to Policy Value Estimation

We next translate the above analysis into the error bounds for policy value estimation.

Corollarry 6.7
.

(1) Under the conditions of
Theorem

6.1
,

|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\|q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\eta_{w,n}C^{*}_{w,n}\mathrm{Err}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{mil},w}.

(2) Under the conditions of
Theorem

6.2
,

|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}-J|\lesssim_{\delta}\|w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\eta_{q,n}C^{*}_{q,n}.

Let us compare Corollary
6.7
to
Theorems

4.1
and
5.5
by focusing on the case of
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{q}
.
For VC classes, both
Theorems

4.1
and
6.7
give
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
. and Corollary
5.5
gives
O(n^{-1/4})
.
For nonparametric models with covering number entropy exponent
\beta<2
,
Theorem

4.1
gives
O(n^{-1/2})
, Corollary
5.5
gives
O(n^{-1/4})
, and Corollary
6.7
gives
O(n^{-1/(2+\beta)})
.
For
\beta\geq 2
,
Theorem

4.1
gives
O(n^{-1/\beta})
and both Corollary
5.5
and
6.7
give
O(n^{-1/(2\beta)})
(when
\beta=2
, we have
\widetilde{O}
).
Therefore, the rate in
Theorem

4.1
is fastest in each case and Corollary
5.5
the slowest, while Corollary
6.7
coincides with the best and worst in different cases.
Under
q
-completeness and
q
-realizability , using no stabilizers yields faster rates than using stabilizers. On the other hand, under
q
-completeness and
q
-realizability , the order is flipped and using a stabilizer yields faster rates than using no stabilizers.

7
First-Order Efficiency Under
w,q
-realizability and
w,q
-completeness

We now study the constant term in the
O(1/\sqrt{n})
rates. We show
\widehat{J}_{wq}^{\mathrm{mil}}
(with sample splitting) can achieve the optimal constant under both realizability and completeness for
w
and
q
.

When we only assume
w
- and
q
-realizability as in
Theorem

4.1
, the dependence of the constant on problem dependent quantities may be poor. For example, when the classes
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{Q}
are both finite, the first-order term of the error
|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}-J|
in
Theorem

4.1
wrt
n
can be upper-bounded by Bernstein’s inequality: with probability
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}-J|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{32\sup_{w\in\mathbb{W},q\in\mathbb{Q}}\mathrm{var}[w(s,a)\{r-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]\times\log(|\mathbb{W}||\mathbb{Q}|/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+O\left(\frac{\log(|\mathbb{W}||\mathbb{Q}|/\delta)}{n}\right).

(8)

This first-order term (the
1/\sqrt{n}-
term) might incur an error characterized by the worst variance term. In this section, we prove that the first-order term is further improved under more conditions in that
\sup_{w\in\mathbb{W},q\in\mathbb{Q}}\mathrm{var}[w\{r-q+q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]
is surprisingly replaced with
\mathrm{var}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\{r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}+\gamma q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}]
. In addition,
\log(|\mathbb{W}||\mathbb{Q}|/\delta)
is replaced with
\log(c/\delta)
, where
c
is some universal constant.
Our proof strategy is to localize around the true
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
by assuming both realizability and completeness.
This is crucial as it means the first-order term matches the asymptotic efficiency bounds of
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
, thus we obtain the
first
finite-sample bound with first-order efficiency in non-tabular environments.

We analyze a sample-splitting version of
\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}
as in
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
;
Kallus and
Uehara 2020a
)
but using MIL estimators for
w
and
q
. The sample-splitting procedure simplifies the analysis of the stochastic equicontinuity term in the error
(
Newey and
Mcfadden 1994
;
Chernozhukov
et al. 2018
)
.
Letting
\phi(s,a,r,s^{\prime};w,q)\coloneqq w(s,a)\{r-q(s,a)+\gamma q(s^{\prime},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})\}+\mathbb{E}_{d_{0}}[q(s_{0},\pi^{\mathrm{e}})]
, the procedure is described as follows:

1.

Split the whole sample into two halves
\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

2.

For
j=0,1
, construct estimators
\widehat{w}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{j},\widehat{q}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{j}
by applying MIL only to the data in
\mathcal{D}_{j}
.

3.

Return
\widetilde{J}_{wq}^{\mathrm{mil}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0,1}\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{D}_{j}\right|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j}}\phi(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s^{\prime}_{i};\widehat{w}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{1-j},\widehat{q}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{1-j})
.

Theorem 7.1

(First-order efficiency)
.

Assume the conditions in
Theorems

6.2
and
6.1
hold. Suppose there exists
C_{\iota,n}
s.t.
\|q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\leq C_{\iota,n}\|\mathcal{B}q-q\|_{2},\,\forall q\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
and
\|w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\leq C_{\iota,n}\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}w-w\|_{2},\,\forall w\in\mathbb{W}_{1}
. Then, with probability
1-(\delta+10\delta^{\prime})
,
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\left|\widetilde{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}-J\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\mathbb{E}[w^{2}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\{r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime})\}^{2}]\log(1/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{1}C_{\iota,n}\eta_{w,n}\eta_{q,n}+\frac{C_{2}C_{\iota,n}(\eta_{w,n}+\eta_{q,n})}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{C_{3}}{n},

(9)

where
C_{1}:=c_{0}C^{*}_{w,n}C^{*}_{q,n}
,
C_{2}:=c_{0}(C^{*}_{w,n}+C^{*}_{q,n}\sqrt{1+C_{\eta}C_{m}})\sqrt{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}
, and
C_{3}:=c_{0}{C_{\mathbb{W}_{1}}C_{\mathbb{Q}_{2}}(\log(1/\delta)+\log(1/\delta^{\prime}))}
.

This theorem yields a first-order efficient bound provided that
C_{\iota,n}\eta_{w,n}\eta_{q,n}=o_{p}(1/\sqrt{n})
and assuming
w
- and
q
-realizability and
w
- and
q
-completeness, as well as
\|q-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\leq C_{\iota,n}\|\mathcal{B}q-q\|_{2}
and
\|w-w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\|_{2}\leq C_{\iota,n}\|\mathcal{B}^{\prime}w-w\|_{2}
. We refer to the latter two conditions as
the
recovery assumption
, which is related to a common condition used in the literature on nonparametric instrumental variable estimation
(
Chen and
Pouzo 2015
;
Blundell et al. 2003
)
. Note
C_{\iota,n}
can depend on
n
.

For example, consider the setting in
Example

9
where we use neural networks for
w
- and
q
-functions to approximate the Sobolev balls
H^{\beta_{1}}_{K_{1}}([0,1]^{d_{1}})
and
H^{\beta_{2}}_{K_{2}}([0,1]^{d_{2}})
respectively. Then, if we additionally assume
C_{\iota,n}=\Omega(1)
, then
Eq.

9
becomes

\sqrt{\frac{2\mathbb{E}[w^{2}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\{r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime})\}^{2}]\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+o_{p}(1/n^{\left(\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{1}+d_{1}}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{2\beta_{2}+d_{2}}\right)\wedge\frac{1}{2}})

This shows that even if one of the function classes is non-Donsker, i.e.,
d_{j}/\beta_{j}\geq 2
for
j=1
or
2
, we can still obtain a parametric rate
O(n^{-1/2})
as long as
\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{1}+d_{1}}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{2\beta_{2}+d_{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. On the other hand, when we only assume
w
- and
q
-realizability in
Theorem

4.1
, if one function class is non-Donsker, we cannot ensure the error bound is
O(n^{-1/2})
, per
Example

3
.

Whenever
C_{\iota,n}\eta_{w,n}\eta_{q,n}=o(n^{-1/2})
(e.g.,
\frac{\beta_{1}}{2\beta_{1}+d_{1}}+\frac{\beta_{2}}{2\beta_{2}+d_{2}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
C_{\iota,n}=\Omega(1)
in the neural network example above), the first-order term with respect to
n
is just the first term in
Eq.

9
, which is much tighter than both
Theorems

4.1
and
7
. The first-order term exactly corresponds to a sub-Gaussian tail with sub-Gaussian variance parameter
\mathrm{EB}/n
where

\displaystyle\mathrm{EB}=\mathbb{E}[w^{2}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)\{r-q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)+v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s^{\prime})\}^{2}],

(10)

which is precisely the asymptotic efficiency bound derived by
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
, Theorem 5)
.
2
2

2

The asymptotic efficiency bound has two meanings. First, it is the minimum limiting variance of
\sqrt{n}(\widehat{J}-J)
among regular estimators
\widehat{J}

(
van der
Vaart 1998
, Theorem 25.20)
. Second, it is a locally minimax lower bound on
\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{J}-J)^{2}]
among all estimators
(
van der
Vaart 1998
, Theorem 25.21)
. In parametric models, such as when the MDP is tabular, it coincides with the Cramér-Rao lower bound.
Although
(
Kallus and
Uehara 2019
)
showed this lower bound can be achieved asymptotically by a general meta-algorithm, their result is asymptotic and assumes estimators are given satisfying certain rates, where achieving these rates is nontrivial and requires realizability and/or completeness assumptions not discussed therein. In their asymptotic result, the higher-order terms may include terms with exponential dependence on problem dependent quantities such as the effective horizon, which is very problematic in RL as discussed in
Section

2
. Similarly, the result of
(
Liao
et al. 2020
)
for the average reward setting (
\gamma=1
) is also asymptotic and may have exponential dependence in higher-order terms. Ours is the first first-order finite-sample result in a general setting with polynomial dependence on problem dependent quantities, and we give explicit estimators.

When the MDP is deterministic, i.e., the transition and reward distributions are deterministic given state-action, our result implies that the rate can be
faster than
O(1/\sqrt{n})
. This is because, in this case,
\mathrm{EB}=0
in
Eq.

10
, so that in
Eq.

9
the first term is
0
. In turn, the error rate is controlled by the higher-order terms. For example, when
\eta_{q,n}
and
\eta_{w,n}
are parametric rates
O(1/\sqrt{n})
and
C_{\iota,n}=O(1)
, the final rate is
O(1/n)
. As far as we know, this type of result is the first in the non-tabular offline setting with general function approximation.

In
Theorem

7.1
, we need the aforementioned recovery assumption to translate Bellman residual errors into
L^{2}
-errors. As in Lemma
2.3
, when
\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}}<1
, we have
C_{\iota,n}=1/(1-\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}})
for any function classes
\mathbb{W}_{1},\mathbb{W}_{2},\mathbb{Q}_{1},\mathbb{Q}_{2}
. By utilizing the structure of function classes, however, the recovery assumption may hold even if
\gamma\sqrt{C_{m}C_{\eta}}\geq 1
under mild assumptions. For example, when we use linear models, the recovery assumption is ensured by more tangible conditions.

Theorem 7.2

(Sufficient conditions for the recovery assumption)
.

Consider linear models
\mathbb{W}_{1}=\mathbb{W}_{2}=\mathbb{Q}_{1}=\mathbb{Q}_{2}=\{\beta^{\top}\phi(s,a);\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, and define
X\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\phi(s,a)\phi(s,a)^{\top}]
.

(1) Suppose
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{2}
, and there exists a matrix
M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
s.t.
\mathcal{T}(\beta^{\top}\phi)=(M^{\top}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\beta)^{\top}\phi
. If
\bar{M}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},X
are non-singular, letting
\bar{M}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}=\gamma M^{\top}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-I
,
the recovery assumption holds with

C_{\iota,n}=\{\sigma_{\max}((\bar{M}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}X\bar{M}^{\top}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})^{-1/2}X(\bar{M}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}X\bar{M}^{\top}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})^{-1/2})\}^{1/2}.

(2) Suppose
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{1}
and there exists a matrix
M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
such that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}(\beta^{\top}\phi)=(M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\beta)^{\top}\phi
. If
\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
,
X
are non-singular, letting
\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}\coloneqq\gamma M^{\prime\top}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}-I
, the recovery assumption holds with

C_{\iota,n}=\{\sigma_{\max}((\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}X(\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})^{\top})^{-1/2}X(\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}X(\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}})^{\top})^{-1/2})\}^{1/2}.

We explain the required assumptions in
Theorem

7.2
. The existence of
M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
essentially implies completeness. Lemma
5.2
ensures the existence of
M_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
in linear MDPs. Lemma
5.4
ensures the existence of
M^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
in an MDPs where the posterior transition density is linear. As in
Section

5
, the non-singularity of
\bar{M}_{\pi},\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi}
is ensured if the feature vectors are stochastic. Thus, the required assumptions are quite reasonable.

In particular, the recovery assumption holds in the tabular case.

Example 10

(First-order efficiency in a tabular case)
.

In a tabular setting, all of the assumptions in
Theorem

7.1
are satisfied from the following lemma. Thus, the first-order lower bound is achieved.

Lemma 7.3
.

If
|\mathcal{S}|<\infty
,
|\mathcal{A}|<\infty
, and
P_{S,A}(s,a)>0,\,\forall(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, then,
\bar{M}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},\bar{M}^{\prime}_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}},X
in
Theorem

7.2
are non-singular by taking
\phi(s,a)
as the standard basis vector in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}||\mathcal{A}|}
.

Finally, we can also obtain the asymptotic statement. This clearly shows the first-order bound is achieved. This can be directly applied to hypothesis testing.

Corollarry 7.4
.

Assume the conditions in
Theorem

7.1
. Suppose
\eta_{w,n}\eta_{q,n}C_{\iota,n}=o(n^{-1/2}),C_{\iota,n}\eta_{w,n}=o(1),C_{\iota,n}\eta_{q,n}=o(1)
with probability
1-\delta
. Then,
\sqrt{n}(\widetilde{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wq}-J)
converges weakly to a Gaussian distribution with mean
0
and variance
\mathrm{EB}
as defined in
Eq.

10
.

8
Extensions

So far, we studied the theoretical properties of state-action-based minimax estimators and their implications to policy evaluation. In this section, we consider some extensions that our analyses admit.
First, we extend our results to state-only-based minimax methods. Then, we discuss how to apply these minimax estimators to policy
optimization
and study the theoretical properties of learned policies.

8.1
State-Based MIL

We now investigate an alternative approach to OPE based on estimating
v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s)
and
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},S}(s)
, rather than
q_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)
and
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)
as discussed so far.

Analogously to
Eq.

3
, we have

\displaystyle J=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim d_{0}}[v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s_{0})]=\mathbb{E}[w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},S}(s)\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)r].

Thus, when we know
\pi^{\mathrm{b}}(a\mid s)
(so that
\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}
is known), then we can also estimate
J
based on estimates of
v_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s)
and
w_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}},S}(s)
. Motivated by the above equation, given function classes
\mathbb{W}_{S1},\mathbb{W}_{S2},\mathbb{V}_{1},\mathbb{V}_{2}
in
[\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}]
, we can consider the state-based MIL estimators:

\displaystyle\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{w,S}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{n}[\widehat{w}_{\mathrm{mil},S}(s)\eta_{\pi^{\mathrm{e}}}(s,a)r],\,\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{v,S}=\mathbb{E}_{d_{0}}[\widehat{v}_{\mathrm{mil},S}(s)],

\displaystyle\widehat{J}^{\mathrm{mil}}_{wv,S}
